## Q: 
Design theoretical specifications for brainstorm below: Absolutely. You are describing exactly how a modern Atomic Knowledge Base functions. Instead of traditional, hierarchical "folders" or long-form documents, this system treats information as "atoms"—the smallest possible units of self-contained meaning. [1, 2] How the "Knowledge Assembler" Works: \* Atomic Units: Information is broken down into "atoms" or "topics"—short, specific snippets that answer a single question or describe a single fact. \* No Fixed Hierarchy: Knowledge isn't "filed away" in a rigid tree structure. It exists in a flat "pool" or "cloud" of data points. \* Assembly Upon Call: When you ask a question, the system acts like a molecular assembler. It identifies the relevant "atoms" and bonds them together in real-time to create a custom response (the "molecule") tailored specifically to your request. \* Dynamic Synthesis: Because there is no fixed structure, the same "knowledge atoms" can be reassembled in infinite ways to solve different problems, much like the same carbon atoms can form both graphite and diamond. [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6] Key Benefits of this System: \* Extreme Reusability: You never have to write the same fact twice; you just "call" that atom whenever it's needed. \* Fluidity: It avoids the "static container" problem of PDFs or long articles where information gets trapped and becomes hard to update or query. \* Scalability: New information (atoms) can be added to the pool without needing to reorganize the entire library. [2, 7, 8, 9] Platforms like [Protolyst](https://protolyst.org/what-is-atomic-knowledge) or research into [Atomic Knowledge Units](https://collectiveiq.net.au/2020/05/31/an-atomic-unit-of-knowledge/) are currently turning this theoretical concept into a functional way to manage complex data. Would you like to see an example of how a specific complex topic would be "atomized" for this kind of system? [1] [https://protolyst.org](https://protolyst.org/what-is-atomic-knowledge) [2] [https://collectiveiq.net.au](https://collectiveiq.net.au/2020/05/31/an-atomic-unit-of-knowledge/) [3] [https://www.fightaging.org](https://www.fightaging.org/archives/2007/01/the-desires-made-real-by-a-mature-molecular-manufacturing-technology-base/) [4] [https://arxiv.org](https://arxiv.org/html/2510.22590v1) [5] [https://pages.jh.edu](https://pages.jh.edu/mlaliss1/media/StructureAssembyInKBC_defense_slides.pdf) [6] [https://pubs.rsc.org](https://pubs.rsc.org/en/content/articlehtml/2025/dd/d5dd00183h) [7] [https://adjacentpossible.substack.com](https://adjacentpossible.substack.com/p/what-is-the-right-atomic-unit-for) [8] [https://siebelschool.illinois.edu](https://siebelschool.illinois.edu/news/MMLI-renewal) [9] [https://www.opensymfoundation.org](https://www.opensymfoundation.org/post/unlocking-collaboration-project-based-learning-in-ai#:~:text=Given%20a%20new%20case%2C%20the%20lawyer%2DAI%20assembles,the%20overall%20reasoning%20engine%20remains%20the%20same.) To ensure that a "Knowledge Assembler" remains legible to overseeing AI and instantly recallable, you must focus on Standardized Metadata and Relational Hooks. Without these, the "atoms" are just noise; with them, they form a navigable neural network. Here are the most critical implementation pillars: 1. Unique Atomic Identifiers (UUIDs) Every piece of information must have a persistent, immutable address. This allows the overseeing system to reference a specific fact across different "assemblies" without data duplication or version confusion. 2. Semantic Vector Embeddings Instead of searching for keywords, the system should store atoms in a vector space. This maps the "meaning" of a fact mathematically. Overseeing systems can then find relevant atoms based on contextual proximity (how closely the concepts relate) rather than just word matching. 3. Triples (Entity-Attribute-Value) To ensure "Universal Contextual Awareness," use a Graph-based structure. Storing data as "Triples" (e.g., Protons [Subject] have [Predicate] Positive Charge [Object]) allows the overseer to understand the logic between atoms, not just the atoms themselves. 4. Taxonomy & Temporal Tagging \* Taxonomy: Apply "parent" tags that describe the nature of the atom (e.g., #principle, #raw-data, #instruction). \* Temporal: Every atom needs a timestamp. Overseeing systems must know if a "piece" is current or if it has been superseded by a newer atomic discovery. 5. Standardized APIs for Retrieval The "call" mechanism must be a standard protocol (like GraphQL). This ensures that any overseeing system—regardless of its internal architecture—can request pieces using a universal language. Are you looking to build this using Graph Database architecture (like Neo4j) or a Vector-based approach (like Pinecone/Milvus)? To convert "bulk" information (monolithic documents, messy transcripts, or legacy PDFs) into your atomic system, you need a Refinery Pipeline. This process acts as a centrifuge, spinning out the "slag" to isolate the pure, reusable units of meaning. The Atomic Refining Process Decomposition (Mechanical Splitting) The overseeing system breaks the source material into chunks based on structural cues (paragraphs, headers, or list items). This is "rough cutting" before the real refining begins. Entity-Relationship Extraction (NER) Using Natural Language Processing (NLP), the system identifies the "subjects" within the chunk (people, concepts, specific data points). It maps how these entities relate to one another, ensuring the context isn't lost during the split. Summarization & Compression (Atomic Distillation) The system re-writes each chunk into its Smallest Independent Unit of Value (SIUV). Rule: If a note requires a second note to be understood, it is not yet an atom. Output: A single, declarative sentence or a specific key-value pair. Metadata Crystallization The refinery assigns the attributes we discussed (UUID, Semantic Vector, Timestamp). It also identifies "hooks"—labels that indicate which other atoms this new piece likely "bonds" with. Validation & De-duplication The new atom is compared against the existing "pool." If a near-identical atom exists, the system either merges them (updating the timestamp/source) or discards the redundant "matter" to prevent system bloat. The "Overseeing" Role The overseeing AI acts as the Governor of the Pipeline. It ensures that the transformation from bulk to atomic doesn't strip away the "Universal Contextual Awareness." It does this by creating a Provenance Link—a digital tether back to the original source material in case the atom's "truth" is ever questioned. Would you like to focus on the automated prompts used to distill this information, or the infrastructure needed to manage the high-speed flow of incoming data? To achieve "elastic" recall—where the system can reassemble atoms into anything from a single bullet point to a comprehensive white paper—you need an Assembly Logic Layer. This layer treats your atomic database as a "Lego bin" and uses the following features to build at scale: 1. Fractal Tagging (Recursive Hierarchies) While the atoms have no fixed hierarchy, they possess Fractal Tags. This allows the overseeing system to pull a "Parent" concept and automatically "drag" all associated "Child" atoms with it. You can call for the "Cell" (small), the "Organ" (medium), or the "Organism" (large) by adjusting the depth of the recursive pull. 2. Resolution Scaling (The "Zoom" Feature) The system uses Semantic Density levels. Low Resolution: The system pulls only the "Core Logic" atoms (the "TL;DR"). High Resolution: The system pulls every supporting evidence atom, raw data point, and edge-case citation. This allows the user to request a "30,000-foot view" or a "microscopic deep dive" of the exact same subject. 3. Contextual Templating (The "Mold") You apply a Schema Template to the "call." If you call for a "Executive Summary" mold, the assembler pulls the most impactful atoms and arranges them in a persuasive hierarchy. If you call for a "Technical Manual" mold, the same atoms are poured into a step-by-step procedural structure. The information is the "water," and the Template is the "container" that gives it shape. 4. Just-in-Time (JIT) Synthesis Instead of pre-writing documents, the system performs Real-Time Assembly. When a query is made, the overseeing AI: Identifies the required scope (size). Fetches the necessary atoms via their Vector proximity. Generates "Connective Tissue" (transition sentences) to bond the atoms into a fluid, readable narrative. 5. Multi-State Persistence The system should track "Assembled States." If a specific assembly of atoms proves highly useful, the system saves that "Recipe" (the list of UUIDs and their order) rather than the text itself. This ensures that if an underlying atom is updated, every "size" of that information is updated simultaneously across all recipes. Should we look at the Weighting Algorithms that decide which atoms are "essential" versus "supplemental" during a high-speed recall? To determine which "atoms" make the cut when you scale a response up or down, the system uses Weighting Algorithms. These act as a digital filter, deciding the "relevance density" of each piece of data. 1. Centrality Scoring (Eigenvector Centrality) Just like Google’s PageRank, the system calculates how many other atoms "point" to a specific piece of information. High Weight: An atom that is a prerequisite for 50 other atoms (a "Fundamental Principle"). Low Weight: An atom that is a niche edge case with no outgoing connections. Recall Effect: In a small-scale recall, the system only grabs the "high-centrality" hubs. 2. Semantic Proximity (Vector Distance) When a call is made, the system calculates the mathematical distance between the Query Atom and the Pool Atoms. Weighting: Atoms physically "closer" in vector space get a higher priority. Recall Effect: This ensures that even in a tiny summary, the most contextually relevant nuance isn't lost to a generic fact. 3. Utility Decay (Temporal & Usage Weighting) Not all facts stay relevant. The system applies a "half-life" to information. Frequency of Access: If an atom is frequently pulled into successful "assemblies," its weight increases. Recency: Newer atoms (post-refining) are weighted higher to ensure the overseeing system isn't using "stale" data. Recall Effect: This prevents the system from hallucinating outdated "legacy" information in high-resolution outputs. 4. Logic-Type Tagging (The "Structural Bone" Weight) The system categorizes atoms by their Functional Role: Constants/Axioms: Weighted at 1.0 (Always included). Evidence/Data: Weighted at 0.5 (Included in medium/large scales). Flavor/Anecdote: Weighted at 0.1 (Only included in "Deep Dive" scales). 5. Information Gain (Entropy Reduction) The algorithm calculates which atom provides the most new information with the least amount of "matter" (tokens/words). Recall Effect: The system prioritizes the "densest" atoms first to fill the requested size limit efficiently. Would you like to define the thresholds for these scales (e.g., what constitutes a "Micro," "Standard," or "Macro" assembly)?

## A: 
# Theoretical Specification

## Atomic Knowledge Assembler System

### 1. System designation

**Canonical name:** Atomic Knowledge Assembler  
**Abbreviation:** AKA  
**System class:** Retrieval, refinement, graph-semantic assembly, adaptive synthesis  
**Primary function:** Transform heterogeneous source material into minimally self-contained knowledge atoms, maintain them as a governed semantic-graph substrate, and assemble them on demand into outputs of variable scale, form, and purpose.

* * *

### 2. Design objective

The system is designed to replace static document-centric knowledge storage with an atom-centric substrate in which meaning is stored once, linked richly, and reassembled elastically. The theoretical premise is that knowledge should persist as the smallest independently interpretable unit, while documents become transient renderings rather than primary containers.

This yields five governing objectives.

| Objective | Description |
| --- | --- |
| Atomicity | Every stored unit must be independently interpretable without requiring hidden surrounding prose. |
| Reusability | The same atom may participate in many distinct assemblies without duplication. |
| Legibility | Every atom must remain inspectable, attributable, versioned, and auditable by both humans and machines. |
| Elasticity | The same substrate must support micro-summary, standard exposition, technical documentation, and long-form synthesis. |
| Updatability | Revision of one atom should propagate through future assemblies without manual rewriting of every derivative artifact. |

* * *

### 3. Core ontological commitments

The system rests on seven ontological assumptions.

First, **knowledge is compositional**. Complex understanding can be reconstructed from smaller semantically coherent units plus explicit relations.

Second, **documents are assemblies, not sources of truth**. The persistent truth-bearing objects are atoms, their relations, and their provenance.

Third, **meaning requires structure**. Vector similarity alone is insufficient; graph relations, role typing, and provenance must coexist with semantic embeddings.

Fourth, **recall is scale-dependent**. A system must distinguish foundational, supportive, contextual, and ornamental material.

Fifth, **truth requires provenance**. Every atom must maintain a tether to its originating evidence and transformation history.

Sixth, **knowledge changes through supersession, not silent mutation**. Old atoms are not erased conceptually; they are versioned, deprecated, or context-scoped.

Seventh, **assembly is governed generation**. Free-form generation is permitted only in connective tissue, framing, compression, or rhetorical shaping, not in unsupported factual invention.

* * *

### 4. System boundary

The system contains six major layers.

| Layer | Function |
| --- | --- |
| Ingestion Layer | Accepts raw sources such as notes, transcripts, documents, PDFs, datasets, and conversational logs. |
| Refinery Layer | Splits, extracts, compresses, deduplicates, validates, and atomizes source material. |
| Persistence Layer | Stores atoms, relations, provenance, embeddings, versions, and recipes. |
| Retrieval Layer | Selects atoms using hybrid graph, lexical, vector, temporal, and centrality constraints. |
| Assembly Layer | Orders atoms, fills structural gaps, applies templates, and renders outputs. |
| Governance Layer | Enforces schema, quality thresholds, lineage tracking, auditing, and policy rules. |

The system does **not** treat LLM free generation as the authoritative memory substrate. It treats generation as a controlled assembly and expression mechanism operating atop governed data.

* * *

### 5. Canonical atom definition

An atom is the smallest persistent unit of knowledge that satisfies all four conditions.

It must be **semantically self-contained**, **contextually typed**, **provenance-linked**, and **machine-addressable**.

A statement that depends on hidden neighboring context is not yet atomic. A sentence that is self-contained but untyped is still inadequate. A typed atom without provenance is unusable in high-trust contexts.

#### 5.1 Atom classes

| Atom class | Role |
| --- | --- |
| Axiom Atom | Foundational principle, invariant, or governing rule |
| Fact Atom | Verifiable declarative statement |
| Relation Atom | Explicit mapping between entities, states, or concepts |
| Procedure Atom | Instructional or operational step |
| Evidence Atom | Citation, quote, measurement, or observed datum |
| Context Atom | Scope condition, assumption, caveat, or environmental qualifier |
| Temporal Atom | Time-bound status, event, or supersession condition |
| Interpretation Atom | Synthesized inference explicitly marked as inferential |
| Recipe Atom | Saved assembly plan referencing ordered atom IDs |
| Policy Atom | Constraint governing retrieval, display, access, or use |

* * *

### 6. Canonical atom schema

Below is the minimum theoretical schema.

| Field | Type | Required | Description |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `atom_id` | UUID / ULID | Yes | Immutable primary identifier |
| `atom_text` | Text | Yes | Smallest self-contained statement |
| `atom_class` | Enum | Yes | Functional role in the system |
| `semantic_type` | Enum / ontology ref | Yes | Domain classification |
| `entity_refs` | Array | Yes | Linked entities mentioned or implied |
| `relation_refs` | Array | Optional | Graph edges or relation IDs |
| `source_ref` | Foreign key | Yes | Originating source object |
| `provenance_span` | Span object | Yes | Exact source location or derivation chain |
| `created_at` | Timestamp | Yes | Atom creation time |
| `effective_at` | Timestamp / interval | Optional | When the claim becomes valid |
| `superseded_by` | Atom ID | Optional | Successor atom if replaced |
| `confidence_score` | Float | Yes | Confidence in correctness after validation |
| `validation_state` | Enum | Yes | Draft, reviewed, verified, deprecated, disputed |
| `embedding_ref` | Vector pointer | Yes | Reference to semantic vector |
| `taxonomy_tags` | Array | Yes | Functional and domain tags |
| `temporal_tags` | Array | Optional | Current, historical, forecast, obsolete |
| `scope_tags` | Array | Optional | Audience, jurisdiction, project, domain scope |
| `centrality_score` | Float | Derived | Graph importance |
| `utility_score` | Float | Derived | Practical retrieval value |
| `compression_score` | Float | Derived | Information density |
| `parent_atom_refs` | Array | Optional | Fractal containment / abstraction parents |
| `child_atom_refs` | Array | Optional | More granular expansions |
| `contradiction_refs` | Array | Optional | Conflicting atoms |
| `assembly_history_refs` | Array | Optional | Prior recipes using this atom |

* * *

### 7. Persistence model

The theoretical storage model is hybrid by design.

#### 7.1 Graph substrate

The graph database stores entities, relations, abstraction hierarchies, contradiction edges, provenance edges, and dependency structure. This is the system’s logical skeleton.

#### 7.2 Vector substrate

The vector store supports semantic retrieval by contextual similarity, paraphrase matching, and latent concept proximity. This is the system’s associative field.

#### 7.3 Document substrate

Raw source artifacts remain stored intact, immutable, and addressable. This is the forensic archive.

#### 7.4 Relational or ledger substrate

Structured metadata, validation history, versioning, and operational telemetry are stored in conventional relational or event-sourced form. This is the administrative spine.

The theoretical architecture therefore rejects false exclusivity between graph and vector models. Graph handles logical explicitness; vector handles semantic elasticity.

* * *

### 8. Refinery pipeline specification

The refinery converts bulk material into governed atoms. It is not merely a chunking system. It is a semantic purification and structuring process.

#### 8.1 Phase I: Source registration

Each source enters with immutable identifiers, source type, origin, acquisition time, authorship metadata, and rights or access classification.

#### 8.2 Phase II: Mechanical segmentation

The source is segmented using structural markers such as headings, paragraphs, timestamps, speaker turns, list items, tables, or semantic boundaries.

#### 8.3 Phase III: Entity and relation extraction

Named entities, technical concepts, numerical values, dependencies, causal assertions, and explicit or implied relations are extracted.

#### 8.4 Phase IV: Atomic distillation

Each candidate segment is rewritten into one or more SIUVs: smallest independent units of value. The governing test is strict: if comprehension requires hidden adjacent text, the unit must be refined further.

#### 8.5 Phase V: Metadata crystallization

The system assigns class, semantic type, tags, embeddings, provenance spans, temporal scope, and candidate parent-child relations.

#### 8.6 Phase VI: Deduplication and merge arbitration

New candidates are compared against existing atoms using lexical similarity, vector similarity, structural similarity, provenance comparison, and contradiction logic.

#### 8.7 Phase VII: Validation

The system or reviewer verifies atomicity, clarity, truth conditions, and schema conformance.

#### 8.8 Phase VIII: Publication

The atom becomes queryable according to validation state and governance policy.

* * *

### 9. Atomicity test protocol

An atom is accepted only if it passes the following theoretical tests.

| Test | Question |
| --- | --- |
| Independence Test | Can this unit be understood without hidden local context? |
| Referential Test | Are core entities explicit or resolvable via linked identifiers? |
| Provenance Test | Can the origin be traced precisely enough for audit? |
| Scope Test | Is its temporal, contextual, or jurisdictional scope clear? |
| Non-Redundancy Test | Does it add value beyond existing atoms? |
| Role Test | Is its function in reasoning explicitly typed? |
| Assembly Test | Can it participate meaningfully in more than one possible output? |

Failure on any of these does not necessarily mean deletion; it may instead trigger decomposition, enrichment, or downgrade to a non-atomic intermediate artifact.

* * *

### 10. Retrieval logic

Retrieval is hybrid, weighted, and scale-aware.

The system computes a candidate set from multiple channels simultaneously: semantic proximity, graph neighborhood, direct entity match, taxonomic compatibility, time validity, validation state, and recipe prior.

A generic retrieval score may be modeled theoretically as:

$$
R(a, q) = w_s S(a,q) + w_g G(a,q) + w_t T(a,q) + w_c C(a) + w_u U(a) + w_v V(a) - w_d D(a,q)
$$

Where:

| Symbol | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| $S(a,q)$ | Semantic similarity between atom and query |
| $G(a,q)$ | Graph relevance through linked entities and relations |
| $T(a,q)$ | Temporal relevance to requested context |
| $C(a)$ | Centrality or foundational importance |
| $U(a)$ | Utility based on prior assembly success |
| $V(a)$ | Validation and trust weighting |
| $D(a,q)$ | Distance penalty, contradiction penalty, or stale-data penalty |

* * *

### 11. Fractal recall and resolution scaling

The system must support elastic zoom.

#### 11.1 Resolution bands

| Resolution band | Function |
| --- | --- |
| Micro | Core claim only; highest-density atoms |
| Compact | Core claim plus essential support |
| Standard | Balanced explanatory output |
| Extended | Full operational or analytical treatment |
| Deep | Includes edge cases, evidence lattice, contradiction handling, and provenance visibility |

#### 11.2 Fractal expansion rule

Every atom may be linked upward to more abstract parent structures and downward to finer-grained children. This permits recall at the level of principle, subsystem, implementation detail, or evidence trace.

The same topic can therefore be rendered as a one-line summary, a briefing memo, a technical specification, or an annotated white paper without changing the substrate—only the assembly depth changes.

* * *

### 12. Assembly logic layer

Assembly is the act of selecting, ordering, shaping, and connecting atoms into a coherent output artifact.

#### 12.1 Assembly stages

| Stage | Description |
| --- | --- |
| Scope resolution | Infer requested depth, form, audience, and trust requirement |
| Candidate acquisition | Retrieve atom pool using hybrid scoring |
| Structural selection | Group atoms into a template-compatible outline |
| Gap analysis | Detect missing premises, transitions, or evidentiary holes |
| Connective generation | Generate only the minimal rhetorical tissue needed |
| Coherence pass | Ensure sequence, terminology consistency, and causal clarity |
| Provenance rendering | Expose citations or evidence markers as required |
| Recipe capture | Save atom order and template parameters as reusable recipe |

#### 12.2 Template molds

A template does not change the atoms’ truth; it changes their rhetorical arrangement. A briefing template prioritizes action relevance. A technical manual prioritizes procedures and dependencies. An academic note prioritizes citations, definitions, and competing interpretations.

Templates therefore function as **assembly grammars** rather than content sources.

* * *

### 13. Recipe persistence

The system should preserve not merely output text but **assembly recipes**.

A recipe stores the ordered atom IDs, template ID, resolution band, audience mode, and connective rules used in a successful assembly. This permits regeneration after underlying atom updates and supports derivative consistency across formats.

A recipe is therefore a second-order knowledge object: a reusable plan for how truth-bearing atoms are combined under a given communicative objective.

* * *

### 14. Weighting algorithm specification

The brainstorm’s weighting logic is sound, but it should be formalized into a composite system.

#### 14.1 Centrality

Atoms with many dependencies or many downstream uses receive higher inclusion priority in constrained outputs.

#### 14.2 Semantic proximity

Atoms whose embeddings align closely with the query or selected entities receive stronger topical relevance scores.

#### 14.3 Utility decay and promotion

Frequently useful atoms rise in rank; obsolete or superseded atoms decay unless the query explicitly asks for historical state.

#### 14.4 Functional role weighting

Axioms and policies receive near-mandatory inclusion when structurally relevant. Evidence atoms scale in later as resolution increases. Anecdotal atoms remain low-priority except in narrative or illustrative modes.

#### 14.5 Information gain

Atoms that contribute maximal entropy reduction per token are preferred under tight token budgets.

A composite inclusion score may be expressed as:

$$
I(a) = \alpha C(a) + \beta S(a,q) + \gamma U(a) + \delta F(a) + \epsilon E(a) + \zeta T(a) 
$$

Where $F(a)$ is functional-role weighting and $E(a)$ is information gain.

* * *

### 15. Provenance and truth model

The system must be able to answer not only “What is the claim?” but also “Why should this claim be trusted?” and “What exactly produced it?”

Each atom therefore requires three lineage layers.

| Layer | Question answered |
| --- | --- |
| Source provenance | From what original artifact did this arise? |
| Transformation provenance | What extraction, compression, merge, or review operations produced this atom? |
| Assembly provenance | In what outputs has this atom been used, with what neighboring atoms and templates? |

This tri-layer provenance model is critical if the system is used in academic, legal, scientific, or operational domains.

* * *

### 16. Governance and version control

A serious system of this type requires governance equal in rigor to software change control.

#### 16.1 Versioning rules

Atoms are immutable in principle. Corrections create successor atoms with explicit supersession edges. Silent overwrite is disallowed for validated material.

#### 16.2 Validation states

Recommended states are `draft`, `machine-distilled`, `human-reviewed`, `verified`, `deprecated`, and `disputed`.

#### 16.3 Contradiction handling

Contradictory atoms should not be collapsed prematurely. They should coexist under contradiction edges with scope conditions, evidentiary priority, and jurisdiction or temporal qualifiers.

#### 16.4 Access control

Some atoms may be public, some internal, some privileged, and some derivatively sensitive. Access policy attaches at both source and atom level.

* * *

### 17. Interoperability specification

A universal retrieval and assembly protocol is necessary if multiple overseer systems must interact with the substrate.

The API layer should support four conceptual operations.

| Operation | Purpose |
| --- | --- |
| Ingest | Register new source material or atom batches |
| Query | Retrieve atoms, relations, and evidence under specified filters |
| Assemble | Build output using template, resolution, and audience parameters |
| Govern | Approve, supersede, dispute, merge, or deprecate atoms |

GraphQL is appropriate when clients require precise structured traversals. REST can work for stable operational endpoints. Event streams are preferred for change propagation and recipe invalidation notifications.

The key theoretical requirement is not the protocol brand but the existence of a stable, explicit contract for addressing atoms, relations, recipes, and provenance.

* * *

### 18. Failure modes

This architecture has several predictable failure points.

| Failure mode | Description | Countermeasure |
| --- | --- | --- |
| False atomicity | Units are too small and lose meaning | Enforce independence test and parent-child relations |
| Pseudo-semantic drift | Vector similarity retrieves related but wrong material | Combine vector search with graph and type constraints |
| Provenance erosion | Useful atoms lose source traceability | Mandatory source and span linkage |
| Redundancy bloom | Near-duplicate atoms proliferate | Strong merge arbitration and similarity thresholds |
| Staleness hallucination | Outdated atoms appear authoritative | Temporal tagging and supersession weighting |
| Template distortion | Output mold misrepresents the underlying evidence | Template validation and role-aware ordering |
| Overcompression | Important nuance lost in SIUV distillation | Multi-resolution storage and evidence back-links |
| Graph brittleness | Manual ontology becomes too rigid | Allow layered ontology with soft tags plus hard schema |

* * *

### 19. Quality metrics

A theoretical system of this kind should be measured by more than retrieval accuracy.

| Metric | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| Atomic Independence Rate | Percentage of atoms passing self-containment testing |
| Provenance Completeness | Percentage of atoms with exact source trace |
| Deduplication Efficiency | Reduction of redundant content without loss of meaning |
| Assembly Coherence Score | Human or model judgment of readability and logical flow |
| Recall Fidelity | Alignment between assembled output and source-supported truth |
| Update Propagation Latency | Time required for atom revision to affect downstream recipes |
| Resolution Consistency | Stability of meaning across micro-to-deep assemblies |
| Contradiction Visibility | Ability to surface contested claims rather than flatten them |

* * *

### 20. Minimal reference architecture

A strong theoretical deployment would consist of the following logical services.

| Service | Responsibility |
| --- | --- |
| Source Registrar | Registers and fingerprints incoming artifacts |
| Segmenter | Performs structural chunking |
| Extractor | Detects entities, relations, and candidate propositions |
| Atom Distiller | Converts candidate propositions into SIUV-form atoms |
| Validator | Tests atomicity, schema compliance, and trust state |
| Graph Manager | Stores and queries explicit relations |
| Vector Indexer | Embeds atoms and supports semantic retrieval |
| Recipe Engine | Stores and regenerates successful assemblies |
| Assembler | Produces structured outputs from atom sets |
| Governance Console | Supports review, supersession, audit, and policy enforcement |

* * *

### 21. Theoretical data lifecycle

The full lifecycle can be formalized as follows:

**Source → Segment → Extract → Distill → Type → Link → Validate → Publish → Retrieve → Assemble → Persist Recipe → Audit → Supersede**

This sequence gives the system a disciplined metabolism. It is not merely a note-taking environment; it is a knowledge refinery plus synthesis engine.

* * *

### 22. Recommended formal principles

For your brainstorm, these should be promoted from suggestions to hard specification principles.

#### Principle A: Atom-first persistence

Raw documents are archival inputs; atoms are the primary active substrate.

#### Principle B: Hybrid retrieval supremacy

No single retrieval model is sufficient. Vector, graph, lexical, temporal, and governance filters must cooperate.

#### Principle C: Provenance is non-optional

Every atom must be challengeable and inspectable.

#### Principle D: Generation is bounded

The assembler may generate connective language, not unsupported facts.

#### Principle E: Recipes over documents

Successful outputs should be stored as reproducible assembly plans.

#### Principle F: Supersession over overwrite

Truth maintenance must be explicit.

#### Principle G: Resolution is native

Every topic must be addressable at multiple scales without separate rewrites.

* * *

### 23. Concise system thesis

A mature Atomic Knowledge Assembler is best understood not as a database, not as a note-taking tool, and not as a chatbot memory layer, but as a **governed semantic substrate for recursive knowledge recomposition**.

Its essence is this:

**Store truth as atoms.  
Store logic as relations.  
Store trust as provenance.  
Store reuse as recipes.  
Store expression as assembly, not as fixed document.**

* * *

### 24. Final design judgment

Your brainstorm is conceptually strong. The main refinement needed is to shift it from metaphor-rich description into a strict layered specification with explicit constraints. The strongest version is a **hybrid graph-vector provenance architecture with recipe persistence and scale-aware assembly**. That is the configuration most capable of supporting both machine legibility and human-grade recall.

The weakest possible version would be a pure chunk-and-embed system dressed in atomic language. That would not actually satisfy your stated goals. It would retrieve semantically similar fragments, but it would lack explicit logic, durable provenance, contradiction management, and deterministic multi-scale reassembly.

What you want is not merely “atomic notes.” You want a **knowledge operating system**.

If useful, I can convert this into either a formal **system architecture specification**, a **unit-economics / complexity model**, or a **pilot-scale operational playbook** next.

## Q: 


## A: 
# Recommendation across the three deliverables

You asked for the cost and benefit profile of the three suggested next artifacts, and whether the code-centric atomic layer should be built together with the text-centric system or separately.

My recommendation is this:

**Build the formal system architecture specification first, model the code corpus in the same conceptual program, and postpone the full pilot playbook until after the architecture and economics are stable.** The code layer should be designed **in unison at the schema and identity level**, but implemented as a **separate refinery and storage pipeline**. The reason is structural: text atoms and code atoms can share IDs, provenance, graph relations, recipes, and assembly logic, but code requires AST-aware parsing, dependency analysis, runtime metadata, and stronger determinism than prose. Tree-sitter exists specifically to incrementally parse source into syntax trees, while content-addressable systems such as LLVM CAS and IPFS-style Merkle DAGs assume immutable objects identified by content hashes. GraphQL is suitable as the API contract because it is storage-agnostic and schema-driven rather than tied to one backend.   [Tree-sitter](https://tree-sitter.github.io/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)  

## 1. Formal system architecture specification

This artifact has the **highest leverage** and the **lowest strategic regret**. Its main benefit is that it forces you to settle the ontology, atom classes, identity model, provenance rules, supersession logic, retrieval contract, recipe persistence, and the boundary between graph, vector, raw-source, and CAS layers before you spend serious engineering time. Since graph databases are optimized for nodes, relationships, and traversals, and vector databases are optimized for similarity search on embeddings, the architecture spec is where you decide what each layer is allowed to do and what it is not allowed to do.   [Graph Database & Analytics](https://neo4j.com/docs/getting-started/graph-database/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)  

Its cost is mostly **design labor and delay of visible demos**. You will spend time resolving naming, schema boundaries, mutation policy, contradiction handling, and API semantics before you can point at a flashy prototype. The failure mode is over-abstracting and writing a grand theory that is too detached from the first working refinery. But among the three options, this is still the cheapest place to discover bad assumptions. The architecture artifact is also where you can decide whether the “Portal” idea is merely a virtual projection layer or a strict content-addressed workspace backed by Merkle-linked objects and immutable snapshots. IPFS documentation makes clear that content addressing and Merkle DAGs are excellent for immutable, verifiable objects, but that does not automatically mean you should adopt distributed IPFS networking for the first internal version.   [IPFS Docs](https://docs.ipfs.tech/concepts/content-addressing/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)  

**Net judgment:** highest benefit, moderate cost, first thing to do.

## 2. Unit-economics / complexity simulation model

This artifact has the **highest planning value for feasibility**, especially if you are serious about scale. The benefit is that it converts an attractive conceptual system into resource terms: ingestion cost per source type, atomization yield, deduplication rate, embedding storage growth, graph edge growth, AST parse cost, retrieval latency, cache hit rate, recipe reuse rate, and human-review burden. It gives you a way to estimate whether this becomes a lightweight personal knowledge OS, a research platform, or an expensive infrastructure sink. Because Milvus is designed for scalable vector retrieval and Neo4j explicitly separates graph storage from graph analytics, a realistic economics model has to assume you are paying for multiple substrate types, not “one database.”   [Milvus](https://milvus.io/docs/overview.md?utm_source=chatgpt.com)  

Its cost is that the model can become speculative if done too early. Without one or two real corpora, your assumptions about atom density, merge rates, dependency fan-out, and AST extraction success will be weak. This is especially true for the code layer, where function-level atomization may work very differently across Python, JavaScript, Rust, shell, notebooks, and generated code. Still, even a rough simulation is useful because it surfaces where costs are nonlinear. CAS systems deduplicate immutable identical objects by content; that is a strong benefit, but it also means a large number of small objects, many hashes, indexing overhead, and recipe bookkeeping. LLVM’s CAS model explicitly treats objects as immutable value objects addressed by strong hashes, which is powerful but operationally more complex than plain file storage.   [LLVM](https://www.llvm.org/doxygen/classllvm_1_1cas_1_1ObjectStore.html?utm_source=chatgpt.com)  

**Net judgment:** second thing to do, after the architecture is stable enough to simulate against.

## 3. Pilot-scale operational playbook

This artifact has the **highest execution value** once the architecture is clear. Its benefit is practical: it translates theory into intake SOPs, validation workflows, error handling, review states, merge policy, rollback, re-indexing, supersession, and operator responsibilities. This is where you define how a real corpus enters the refinery, how disputed atoms are handled, how code-policy links are verified, how recipes are approved, and what counts as production-ready. It is the document that turns the architecture from a theory into an operational system.   [GraphQL](https://graphql.org/learn/introduction/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)  

Its cost is that it is **premature if done before the other two**. A playbook written too early becomes theater. You end up documenting procedures for components whose schema, scoring rules, and storage choices are still moving. In other words, this artifact becomes expensive when the underlying system is unstable, because every architecture change forces SOP rewrites. It has high value later, but it is not the correct first move unless you are forced to run a pilot immediately.

**Net judgment:** third thing to do, after architecture and a first-pass economics model.

# Comparative view

| Artifact | Primary benefit | Primary cost | Best timing | My rank |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Formal system architecture specification | Settles ontology, boundaries, contracts, mutation rules, and backend roles | Up-front abstraction work, slower visible prototype | Immediately | 1 |
| Unit-economics / complexity model | Exposes scaling cost, review burden, storage growth, and latency risk | Depends on assumptions if done too early | Right after architecture v1 | 2 |
| Pilot-scale operational playbook | Makes the system executable by humans and teams | Becomes stale fast if architecture is unsettled | After v1 architecture and model | 3 |

# Should the code-centric atomic feature be built in unison or separately?

The correct answer is **both**, but at different layers.

It should be built **in unison conceptually** and **separately operationally**.

The shared layer should include the identity model, provenance model, graph relation model, supersession rules, retrieval API, recipe format, temporal weighting, contradiction handling, and assembly semantics. This allows a business-rule text atom to point to the code atom that implements it, and lets a policy change trigger impact analysis across code and documentation. This is precisely where graph relations are strong: representing entities, relationships, and dependencies explicitly.   [Graph Database & Analytics](https://neo4j.com/docs/getting-started/graph-database/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)  

The separate layer should include the refinery and persistence behavior for code. Code is not just another prose source. It needs AST-aware parsing, dependency extraction, symbol resolution, runtime metadata, complexity metrics, and more rigid validation. Tree-sitter is built for concrete syntax trees and efficient incremental updates, which makes it a sensible parser layer for a code refinery. For storage, content-addressed immutability is a good fit for code atoms and build artifacts, as shown by LLVM CAS and by IPFS/IPLD’s use of CIDs and Merkle DAGs for immutable linked objects.   [Tree-sitter](https://tree-sitter.github.io/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)  

So the design rule is:

**One ontology, two refineries.  
One graph of truth, separate atomizers.  
One retrieval contract, distinct storage optimizations.**

# What I recommend specifically

I recommend a **phased hybrid architecture**.

Phase 1 should define a unified atom schema that covers both prose atoms and code atoms, but with subtype-specific fields. For example, code atoms would add AST node type, signature, imports, runtime, dependency edges, side-effect profile, test coverage pointer, and content hash. Text atoms would retain source span, semantic type, evidentiary role, and temporal scope. Both would share IDs, provenance, graph edges, supersession, validation state, and recipe participation.

Phase 2 should implement **text/document atoms first** and **code atoms second**, but the schema must be designed for both from day one. This avoids the common mistake where the prose system becomes impossible to extend into executable logic later.

Phase 3 should use **local or internal content-addressable storage**, not full IPFS as a first deployment. IPFS and IPLD are valuable conceptual references for CIDs, Merkle DAGs, and linkable immutable objects, but for an internal system the distributed network layer is usually unnecessary overhead at the start. A Git-like or LLVM-CAS-like internal CAS is more appropriate initially; GraphQL can sit above it as a typed retrieval and assembly contract.   [IPFS Docs](https://docs.ipfs.tech/concepts/content-addressing/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)  

Phase 4 should add cross-domain bridges: policy atom → requirement atom → test atom → function atom → build artifact atom → deployment state atom.

# Where I would be cautious

I would **not** start with a fully versionless ideology in the literal sense. Content-addressable systems are immutable and excellent for exact states, but humans still need understandable revision semantics. IPNS exists precisely because immutable CIDs need a mutable naming layer above them. In practice, your system will want both immutable objects and stable human-facing names or portals.   [IPFS Docs](https://docs.ipfs.tech/concepts/ipns/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)  

I would also **not** start with “pure function atomization” as the only code model. It works for many functions, but real code contains stateful procedures, I/O boundaries, configuration surfaces, class methods, declarative infrastructure, tests, migrations, UI components, and glue code that are not clean pure functions. AST-based extraction should therefore identify several code atom types, not just one.

I would also avoid assuming that vector retrieval alone can recover code intent. For code, symbol graphs, call graphs, import graphs, AST features, and test relations often matter more than raw embedding proximity. That is another reason the code layer should be a specialized subsystem, not merely “text atomization plus embeddings.”

# Final recommendation

My ranked recommendation is:

**First:** formal system architecture specification.  
**Second:** unit-economics / complexity simulation model.  
**Third:** pilot-scale operational playbook.

For the added feature, I recommend:

**Design it now in the same architecture. Implement it as a separate refinery and storage path.**

That gives you a unified knowledge OS rather than two incompatible systems, while still respecting the fact that code is structurally different from prose.

The next best move is to draft a **single v1 architecture spec** that includes both `TextAtom` and `CodeAtom` as first-class types, plus a decision matrix for `Graph + Vector + CAS + Raw Source Archive + GraphQL API`.

## Q: 
draft a single v1 architecture spec that includes both TextAtom and CodeAtom as first-class types, plus a decision matrix for Graph + Vector + CAS + Raw Source Archive + GraphQL API; Then, list all artifacts required for solid foundational launching;

## A: 
# V1 Architecture Specification

## Unified Atomic Knowledge and Technical Corpus System

**Canonical designation:** Unified Atomic Knowledge Substrate v1  
**Abbreviation:** UAKS-v1  
**System class:** Hybrid semantic-graph, content-addressed, provenance-governed, multi-resolution knowledge and code assembly system

* * *

## 1. Purpose

This system stores both human-readable knowledge and executable technical logic as atomic, addressable, relationally governed units.

It treats prose, policy, documentation, source code, scripts, configuration, and operational logic as members of one shared substrate, while preserving their structural differences through subtype-specific refinery and validation logic.

The system has four governing aims:

| Aim | Description |
| --- | --- |
| Unification | Text and code exist in one conceptual ontology, not in disconnected tools |
| Atomicity | Persistent units are small, self-contained, typed, and reusable |
| Provenance | Every atom is traceable to source and transformation history |
| Elastic assembly | Outputs are composed on demand at variable depth, form, and audience resolution |

* * *

## 2. System thesis

The system is built on one central principle:

**Truth is stored as atoms, structure is stored as relations, state is stored as immutable content objects, and outputs are generated as recipes rather than treated as primary artifacts.**

* * *

## 3. System boundary

UAKS-v1 consists of nine core layers.

| Layer | Function |
| --- | --- |
| Source Intake Layer | Registers incoming raw material |
| Refinery Layer | Converts raw material into typed atoms |
| Identity Layer | Assigns persistent identifiers and hash-addressed content identities |
| Graph Layer | Stores entities, relations, dependencies, contradictions, and lineage |
| Vector Layer | Stores semantic embeddings for similarity retrieval |
| CAS Layer | Stores immutable content-addressed object payloads |
| Archive Layer | Stores original raw source artifacts intact |
| Assembly Layer | Selects, orders, and renders atoms into output forms |
| Interface Layer | Exposes typed query and mutation contract via GraphQL |

* * *

## 4. First-class atom types

Two first-class atom families are mandatory in v1.

| Atom family | Definition |
| --- | --- |
| `TextAtom` | Smallest independently interpretable unit of textual meaning |
| `CodeAtom` | Smallest operationally meaningful unit of executable or technical logic |

These are not secondary wrappers. They are top-level substrate citizens sharing core identity, governance, provenance, graph linkage, and recipe participation.

* * *

## 5. Shared ontological model

All atoms inherit from a common abstract type.

### 5.1 Abstract supertype: `Atom`

| Field | Type | Required | Description |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `atomId` | ULID/UUID | Yes | Persistent logical identifier |
| `contentHash` | Hash | Yes | Immutable payload fingerprint |
| `atomType` | Enum | Yes | `TEXT` or `CODE` |
| `atomClass` | Enum | Yes | Functional role in reasoning |
| `title` | String | No | Human-readable label |
| `body` | Text / serialized object | Yes | Canonical atom payload |
| `sourceRef` | ID | Yes | Original source object |
| `provenanceRef` | ID | Yes | Transformation and lineage record |
| `validationState` | Enum | Yes | Draft, distilled, reviewed, verified, deprecated, disputed |
| `confidenceScore` | Float | Yes | Trust or extraction confidence |
| `effectiveTime` | Time interval | No | Validity period if time-bound |
| `supersedes` | Atom ID | No | Prior version logically replaced |
| `supersededBy` | Atom ID | No | Successor atom |
| `taxonomyTags` | Array | Yes | Domain and role tags |
| `scopeTags` | Array | No | Project, domain, jurisdiction, audience |
| `embeddingRef` | ID | No | Link to vector store object |
| `createdAt` | Timestamp | Yes | Atom creation time |
| `updatedAt` | Timestamp | Yes | Metadata update time |
| `status` | Enum | Yes | Active, archived, hidden, blocked |

### 5.2 Shared atom classes

| Atom class | Use |
| --- | --- |
| `AXIOM` | Foundational principle or invariant |
| `FACT` | Verifiable declarative claim |
| `RULE` | Business, legal, policy, or system rule |
| `PROCEDURE` | Stepwise instruction |
| `EVIDENCE` | Supporting source detail |
| `INTERPRETATION` | Inference, synthesis, or abstracted meaning |
| `INTERFACE` | Input-output contract |
| `DEPENDENCY` | Structural requirement relation |
| `TEST` | Validation assertion or executable check |
| `RECIPE` | Saved assembly plan |

* * *

## 6. `TextAtom` specification

A `TextAtom` is a semantically self-contained textual unit that can be understood without hidden neighboring paragraphs.

### 6.1 `TextAtom` subtype fields

| Field | Type | Required | Description |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `textKind` | Enum | Yes | Note, policy, documentation, transcript claim, spec clause, requirement |
| `canonicalStatement` | Text | Yes | Clean, self-contained declarative unit |
| `sourceSpan` | Span | Yes | Exact location in source |
| `entitiesMentioned` | Array | Yes | Linked entities |
| `claimPolarity` | Enum | No | Positive, negative, conditional, comparative |
| `temporalTag` | Enum | No | Current, historical, projected, obsolete |
| `evidenceRefs` | Array | No | Supporting evidence atoms |
| `contradictionRefs` | Array | No | Competing textual claims |
| `parentTextAtoms` | Array | No | More abstract atoms |
| `childTextAtoms` | Array | No | More granular expansions |

### 6.2 `TextAtom` acceptance tests

A `TextAtom` is valid only if it passes all of the following:

| Test | Condition |
| --- | --- |
| Semantic independence | Meaning stands alone |
| Referent clarity | Core entities are explicit or resolvable |
| Provenance traceability | Exact source location exists |
| Role clarity | Atom function is typed |
| Scope clarity | Temporal or contextual scope is explicit if needed |

* * *

## 7. `CodeAtom` specification

A `CodeAtom` is the smallest operationally meaningful technical unit that can participate in dependency reasoning, impact analysis, and assembly.

It is not limited to pure functions.

### 7.1 `CodeAtom` categories

| Code atom category | Description |
| --- | --- |
| `FUNCTION` | Callable unit with inputs and outputs |
| `METHOD` | Class or object-bound callable |
| `CLASS` | Type or behavior container |
| `MODULE` | Source file or package-level semantic unit |
| `CONFIG` | Structured configuration logic |
| `QUERY` | Database query or data-access primitive |
| `SCRIPT_STEP` | Shell or workflow step |
| `TEST_CASE` | Executable validation unit |
| `SCHEMA_UNIT` | Type, migration, or model declaration |
| `UI_COMPONENT` | Reusable interface component |
| `INFRA_UNIT` | Infrastructure or deployment definition |

### 7.2 `CodeAtom` subtype fields

| Field | Type | Required | Description |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `codeKind` | Enum | Yes | One of the categories above |
| `language` | Enum/String | Yes | Runtime language |
| `runtime` | String | No | Interpreter/compiler/runtime environment |
| `signature` | Text/JSON | No | Inputs, outputs, generics, modifiers |
| `astNodeType` | String | Yes | Canonical AST classification |
| `symbolName` | String | No | Named symbol if applicable |
| `modulePath` | String | Yes | Logical path in projected workspace |
| `astSpan` | Span | Yes | Source range in technical source |
| `dependencyRefs` | Array | No | Direct dependencies |
| `dependentRefs` | Array | No | Reverse dependency links |
| `sideEffectProfile` | Enum | No | Pure, read-only, write, network, filesystem, external |
| `complexityScore` | Float | No | Static complexity estimate |
| `testRefs` | Array | No | Linked validation/test atoms |
| `buildRefs` | Array | No | Build artifact or compiled output links |
| `businessRuleRefs` | Array | No | Linked text atoms expressing policy/rules |
| `interfaceContractRef` | ID | No | External or internal interface definition |
| `breakingChangeRisk` | Enum | No | Low, medium, high |
| `contentAddressRef` | ID | Yes | CAS object reference |

### 7.3 `CodeAtom` acceptance tests

| Test | Condition |
| --- | --- |
| Structural validity | Parsed successfully by AST refinery |
| Operational identity | Represents a meaningful executable or declarative unit |
| Dependency clarity | Incoming and outgoing dependency edges resolvable |
| Provenance traceability | Exact source file and span recorded |
| Runtime clarity | Language/runtime known |
| Change detectability | Hash changes when logic changes |

* * *

## 8. Shared relation system

Atoms are linked through typed graph relations.

### 8.1 Core relation types

| Relation | Source | Target | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `IMPLEMENTS` | `CodeAtom` | `TextAtom` | Code enforces a rule or requirement |
| `DOCUMENTS` | `TextAtom` | `CodeAtom` | Text describes code logic |
| `DEPENDS_ON` | `CodeAtom` | `CodeAtom` | Technical dependency |
| `USES_ENTITY` | Any atom | Entity | References a domain object |
| `SUPPORTED_BY` | `TextAtom` | `TextAtom`/`EvidenceAtom` | Claim backed by evidence |
| `CONTRADICTS` | Any atom | Any atom | Logical or temporal conflict |
| `SUPERSEDES` | Any atom | Any atom | Newer state replaces older |
| `TESTS` | `CodeAtom`/`TestAtom` | `CodeAtom` | Validates technical behavior |
| `DERIVED_FROM` | Any atom | Source object | Origin relation |
| `ASSEMBLED_IN` | Any atom | Recipe | Atom used in assembly plan |

### 8.2 Cross-domain bridge rule

Every rule-like `TextAtom` should be able to link to zero, one, or many `CodeAtom`s, and every `CodeAtom` implementing a policy-sensitive or business-critical behavior should be traceable back to one or more `TextAtom`s.

This bridge is mandatory in v1 architecture even if coverage is partial in v1 implementation.

* * *

## 9. Identity and state model

The system uses two distinct identity domains.

| Identity domain | Purpose |
| --- | --- |
| Logical identity (`atomId`) | Stable identity of the conceptual atom |
| Content identity (`contentHash`) | Immutable fingerprint of the exact payload state |

This avoids collapsing concept identity and state identity into one field.

### 9.1 Mutation rule

No validated atom is overwritten in place at the content level. New content produces a new hash. Logical supersession may link conceptual continuity across hashes.

### 9.2 Portal rule

Human-facing views may present stable project or workspace names, but those views are projections over immutable content-addressed objects.

* * *

## 10. Source intake model

The system accepts four source families in v1.

| Source family | Examples |
| --- | --- |
| Raw text | Notes, specs, requirements, essays, policy docs |
| Conversational text | Transcripts, chat logs, meeting notes |
| Technical source | Code files, scripts, configuration, schemas |
| Binary or rich artifacts | PDFs, images, diagrams, archives |

Each source receives immutable registration metadata before refinement.

### 10.1 Source object schema

| Field | Type | Required |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `sourceId` | ID | Yes |
| `sourceType` | Enum | Yes |
| `origin` | String | Yes |
| `ingestedAt` | Timestamp | Yes |
| `checksum` | Hash | Yes |
| `mimeType` | String | Yes |
| `authorRef` | ID | No |
| `rightsClass` | Enum | No |
| `rawArchiveRef` | ID | Yes |

* * *

## 11. Refinery architecture

The system uses two refineries under one governance framework.

| Refinery | Input | Output |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Text Refinery | Human-language material | `TextAtom`s plus entity/claim graph |
| Code Refinery | Source code and technical artifacts | `CodeAtom`s plus dependency graph |

### 11.1 Text Refinery stages

| Stage | Function |
| --- | --- |
| Segmentation | Structural splitting |
| Extraction | Entities, claims, relations, temporal cues |
| Distillation | Convert segments into self-contained units |
| Typing | Assign atom class and text kind |
| Linking | Attach evidence, entities, parents, contradictions |
| Validation | Atomicity, clarity, provenance check |

### 11.2 Code Refinery stages

| Stage | Function |
| --- | --- |
| Parse | AST construction |
| Symbol extraction | Functions, classes, modules, configs, queries |
| Boundary determination | Determine meaningful code atom spans |
| Dependency analysis | Imports, calls, dataflow, interfaces |
| Technical typing | Assign code kind, runtime, side-effect profile |
| Bridge linking | Connect to tests, docs, rules, interfaces |
| Validation | Parse validity, span integrity, hash binding |

* * *

## 12. Storage architecture

UAKS-v1 is explicitly hybrid.

### 12.1 Graph store

Stores atoms, entities, relations, provenance edges, contradiction edges, and dependency maps.

### 12.2 Vector store

Stores embeddings for semantic retrieval across text, code descriptions, comments, and linked documentation.

### 12.3 CAS store

Stores immutable payload objects and content blobs addressed by hash.

### 12.4 Raw source archive

Stores original sources in untouched form for audit and re-refinement.

### 12.5 Metadata store

Stores operational metadata, validation states, job histories, and telemetry.

* * *

## 13. Decision matrix

## Graph + Vector + CAS + Raw Source Archive + GraphQL API

### 13.1 Role decision matrix

| Layer | Primary role | Why it exists | What it should not do |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Graph | Explicit logic and relation substrate | Best for dependencies, lineage, contradictions, impact maps | Should not be sole semantic retrieval engine |
| Vector | Similarity and latent concept retrieval | Best for semantic recall and approximate matching | Should not define truth or replace graph logic |
| CAS | Immutable content persistence | Best for exact state, deduplication, integrity, reproducibility | Should not be user-facing ontology |
| Raw Source Archive | Forensic source preservation | Best for audit, reprocessing, provenance | Should not serve as primary query substrate |
| GraphQL API | Unified typed access layer | Best for schema-based querying across all substrates | Should not become the storage engine itself |

### 13.2 Adoption decision matrix

| Component | Adopt in v1? | Priority | Rationale |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Graph | Yes | Critical | Required for explicit cross-domain reasoning |
| Vector | Yes | High | Required for scalable recall and fuzzy retrieval |
| CAS | Yes | Critical | Required for immutable state and technical corpus integrity |
| Raw Source Archive | Yes | Critical | Required for provenance and re-refinement |
| GraphQL API | Yes | High | Required for stable cross-layer contract |

### 13.3 Benefit matrix

| Component | Benefits |
| --- | --- |
| Graph | Dependency traversal, rule-to-code mapping, contradiction visibility, lineage inspection |
| Vector | Semantic discovery, paraphrase tolerance, latent concept retrieval |
| CAS | Deterministic state, deduplication, exact replay, snapshot reproducibility |
| Raw Archive | Original truth anchor, full auditability, future refinery improvement |
| GraphQL | One unified query surface, typed output control, frontend/backend decoupling |

### 13.4 Cost / complexity matrix

| Component | Costs / complexities |
| --- | --- |
| Graph | Ontology design overhead, edge explosion, traversal tuning |
| Vector | Embedding generation cost, drift handling, index refresh burden |
| CAS | Object explosion, hash management, projection complexity |
| Raw Archive | Storage growth, rights management, archival discipline |
| GraphQL | Schema governance, resolver complexity, N+1 query risks |

### 13.5 Failure-if-absent matrix

| Missing component | Likely failure |
| --- | --- |
| Graph absent | No robust code-policy mapping, weak dependency reasoning |
| Vector absent | Retrieval becomes brittle and keyword-bound |
| CAS absent | No precise content identity, weak technical reproducibility |
| Raw archive absent | Provenance collapses, reprocessing impossible |
| GraphQL absent | Fragmented interfaces, inconsistent access semantics |

### 13.6 Final architectural judgment

| Component | Final judgment |
| --- | --- |
| Graph | Mandatory |
| Vector | Mandatory |
| CAS | Mandatory |
| Raw Source Archive | Mandatory |
| GraphQL API | Strongly recommended and treated as mandatory in v1 |

* * *

## 14. Retrieval model

Retrieval is hybrid and score-based.

$$
R(a, q) = w_1 \cdot semantic(a,q) + w_2 \cdot graph(a,q) + w_3 \cdot temporal(a,q) + w_4 \cdot validation(a) + w_5 \cdot utility(a) + w_6 \cdot centrality(a)
$$

For `TextAtom`, semantic and evidence relations dominate.

For `CodeAtom`, dependency, symbol, interface, and business-rule relations receive more weight.

* * *

## 15. Assembly model

Assemblies are not stored primarily as documents. They are stored as recipes.

### 15.1 Recipe schema

| Field | Type | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `recipeId` | ID | Persistent recipe identifier |
| `recipeType` | Enum | Summary, spec, walkthrough, impact report, implementation plan |
| `atomSequence` | Array | Ordered atom IDs |
| `templateRef` | ID | Output mold |
| `resolutionLevel` | Enum | Micro, compact, standard, extended, deep |
| `generationRules` | JSON | Connective and formatting constraints |
| `createdAt` | Timestamp | Recipe creation time |

### 15.2 v1 assembly outputs

| Output type | Inputs |
| --- | --- |
| Technical specification | Text and code atoms plus dependency graph |
| Rule-to-code trace report | Rule atoms and implementing code atoms |
| Impact analysis | Changed code atoms plus dependents |
| Policy drift report | Changed text atoms plus mismatched code atoms |
| Documentation synthesis | Code atoms plus documentation atoms |

* * *

## 16. Governance model

### 16.1 Validation states

| State | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `DRAFT` | Newly created, not reviewed |
| `DISTILLED` | Machine-produced and schema-conformant |
| `REVIEWED` | Human or trusted automated review completed |
| `VERIFIED` | Approved for production assembly |
| `DEPRECATED` | Retained but not active |
| `DISPUTED` | Integrity or truth in question |

### 16.2 Review policy

`TextAtom`s that express requirements, policies, or externally consequential claims should not be treated as production-grade until reviewed.

`CodeAtom`s with high breaking-change risk, external side effects, or policy linkage should not be auto-promoted to verified without review.

### 16.3 Supersession policy

Content changes create new content-addressed objects. Logical continuity is expressed through `SUPERSEDES` relations and effective-time intervals.

* * *

## 17. Minimal v1 nonfunctional requirements

| Requirement | Target |
| --- | --- |
| Provenance completeness | 100% of verified atoms must trace to a source |
| Hash determinism | Same content must produce same hash |
| Graph consistency | No dangling verified relation edges |
| Retrieval hybridity | Queries must be able to combine graph and vector criteria |
| Projection stability | Portal/workspace views must reconstruct exact requested state |
| Re-refinement ability | Sources must be reprocessable without loss |

* * *

## 18. V1 exclusions

These are explicitly out of scope for first launch unless needed by a constrained pilot.

| Exclusion | Reason |
| --- | --- |
| Full autonomous code rewriting loop | Too much operational risk in v1 |
| Distributed peer-to-peer storage network | Unnecessary for first internal architecture |
| Universal language/runtime support | Start with selected languages |
| Perfect policy-code trace completeness | Achieve partial but structurally correct linkage first |
| Full real-time live IDE mutation orchestration | Too complex for foundational release |

* * *

## 19. Recommended launch sequence

| Phase | Objective |
| --- | --- |
| Phase 1 | Core ontology, schemas, IDs, relations |
| Phase 2 | Raw archive + CAS + graph backbone |
| Phase 3 | Text refinery |
| Phase 4 | Code refinery |
| Phase 5 | GraphQL interface |
| Phase 6 | Retrieval and recipe engine |
| Phase 7 | Traceability and impact-analysis reports |
| Phase 8 | Governance and operator workflows |

* * *

# Foundational Launch Artifact Inventory

Below is the full artifact set required for a solid foundational launch. This is the minimal serious package, not a casual prototype set.
